=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are N squares arranged in a row, labeled 1, 2, \ldots, N from left to right.
You are given M pairs of integers (L_1, R_1), \ldots, (L_M, R_M).
A square j is defined to be bad if and only if there exists some i such that L_i \leq j \leq R_i.
Determine whether you can move from square 1 to square N by repeatedly performing the following action:

- Let your current square be x. Choose an integer i that satisfies all of the following conditions, and move to square x + i.
- A \leq i \leq B
- x + i \leq N
- Square x + i is not bad.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M A B
L_1 R_1
L_2 R_2
\vdots
L_M R_M

Output

If it is possible to reach square N by repeating the action described in the problem statement, print Yes. Otherwise, print No.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 10^{12}
- 0 \leq M \leq 2 \times 10^4
- 1 \leq A \leq B \leq 20
- 1 < L_i \leq R_i < N \ (1 \leq i \leq M)
- R_i < L_{i+1} \ (1 \leq i \leq M - 1)
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

24 2 3 5
7 8
17 20

Sample Output 1

Yes

You can move to square N in this way: 1 \to 6 \to 9 \to 12 \to 16 \to 21 \to 24.

Sample Input 2

30 1 5 8
4 24

Sample Output 2

No

Sample Input 3

100 4 10 11
16 18
39 42
50 55
93 99

Sample Output 3

Yes

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's design robust algorithm.

We have good positions 1..N, bad blocks [L,R] sorted, disjoint, non-adjacent after merge (L_next > R+1). Need know reachability of positions.

We can process blocks in order. Let current position pointer cur = 1? We want determine if we can reach the start of next block's left vicinity, and cross it.

Because step sizes small, if there is a long good segment, reachability becomes "all positions" after some transient. We can compute reachability for positions up to min(next_block_L-1, some threshold) using DP, and if segment extends beyond threshold, we know all positions from threshold to before block are reachable. Then crossing can be determined.

But we also need reach N after last block. We can similarly check if N reachable in final good segment.

Let's formalize with DP over last B positions.

Let reachable[p] boolean for p in 1..N. For p bad, reachable=False. For p good, reachable[p] = OR_{i=A..B} reachable[p-i], with reachable[p-i]=False if p-i<1 or bad. Starting reachable[1]=True (1 is good). Need reachable[N].

Our DP simulation over positions naturally handles jumps over multiple bad intervals because when computing reachable[y] for a good y, we look back at reachable[x] for x in [y-B,y-A], regardless of bad positions between. Maybe a better approach: Since B<=20 and bad intervals M=2e4, we can process all "interesting" positions: around bad intervals and N. We can maintain reachability of positions in a sliding window, but skip long good stretches by noting if all positions before next bad block are reachable, then we can compute reachability of positions after the block using the formula, without simulating through the block. But if there are multiple close blocks, we may need simulate the gap between them because reachable set after block may be a range of positions, not just all positions.

Let's step back.

We can model the reachable set as a subset of positions. Because B<=20, perhaps we can maintain reachability only for the last B positions before current scan position. To skip a long good segment, if the window becomes all True, then all positions in the segment are reachable; we can advance to the end of the segment (before next bad block) with window all True. Then process the next bad block: we need compute reachability for positions after the block. We can do this by considering the block and the following good segment. If the following good segment is long, we may again get all True. But if the following good segment is short, we simulate.

Could process blocks one by one with a function that, given reachability state before a bad block (specifically, reachability of positions up to L-1), computes reachability state after the block (positions from R+1 onward) by applying the recurrence across the bad block. Since bad positions are unreachable, we can simulate positions L..R (all bad -> reachable false) and then continue into the good segment after R. But R-L+1 could be up to B-1 (if len>=B impossible? Wait if len>=B maybe still possible to jump over multiple blocks, but a single block of len>=B cannot be crossed in one jump because min distance len+1 >B. However, if len>=B, could there be a path that enters the block? No, cannot land inside. To get from before to after, one jump must cross it. Distance from any x<=L-1 to any y>=R+1 is at least len+1 > B. So impossible. Thus len<=B-1 for every merged block. So block length small <=19. Great! We can simulate through bad block positions (at most 19 each) and a few good positions after. This is feasible. But wait if intervals are adjacent, merged block length could be sum of many adjacent intervals, maybe >B; then impossible. If not merged, individual len small but combined adjacent >B; merging handles. So each bad block length <=B-1<=19. Thus simulating through bad blocks is cheap.

But what about long good segments? We can skip when all True. But if A=B, all True never occurs; however A=B means fixed jump length. Need handle separately. For A<B, all True occurs after at most ~400 positions in a good segment. So we can simulate good segment until either all True or reach its end. Since each block length small and M 2e4, simulating up to 400 per gap = 8e6, okay. But we also need simulate through bad blocks (<=19 each) and maybe a few positions after. 8e6 fine in Python.

Need be careful: If a good segment length is huge but all True occurs, we can jump to end of segment. But if there are bad blocks, after jumping to end (L-1), window all True. Then to process next bad block, we need simulate positions L..R (bad) and then after R. But if we simulate bad positions, window will contain false for bad positions; after block, the window may not be all True. We then simulate following good segment until all True or end. This seems correct and simple.

But wait: If we skip a long good segment by setting all positions up to L-1 reachable, we also need ensure that positions in the skipped segment are indeed reachable. We stop when window all True at some position p. Then all future positions in the same good segment are reachable, so yes. We can set current position to L-1 and window all True (last B positions before L-1 are all reachable). But what if L-1 < p+B? The window all True at p means positions p-B+1..p reachable. If we jump to L-1, positions L-B+1..L-1? Are they all reachable? Since all positions between p+1 and L-1 are reachable, yes. So window all True. Good.

Now, is it always safe to stop at all True and jump to end of segment? Suppose there is a bad block immediately after the segment. The last B positions before L-1 all reachable. Good.

Then simulate bad block:
- For p from L to R: p is bad, reachable[p]=False. Update window (drop oldest, append False). We don't need compute OR.
- Then continue with p=R+1, which is good (if R+1 < next L or N). Compute reachable using window (which contains some positions before block and bad positions). This naturally allows jumping from before block to R+1 if distance in [A,B]. If not reachable, maybe later positions after block reachable via jumps from before block or from newly reached positions. We simulate good positions after R until all True or next block. Since block length small, this handles crossing.

But what if a jump from before block lands beyond the next bad block, skipping it? Our sequential simulation will not consider positions beyond the next bad block until we process that next block. However, when simulating good positions after R, we only go up to next_L-1. If a position y beyond next block is reachable directly from before first block, we won't compute it because we stop at next block. But if y is beyond next block, then to reach N we eventually need cross next block anyway. If y is reachable beyond next block, then we could skip processing next block? But if we stop at next block, we might think we need to cross it, but actually we already have a reachable position beyond it. Does our algorithm miss that? Let's examine.

Suppose bad blocks B1=[L1,R1], B2=[L2,R2], with gap. If from a reachable x before B1, we can jump to y > R2 (after B2) in one jump. Then B2 is irrelevant; we are already past it. Our simulation of positions after R1 up to L2-1 won't include y because y > L2-1 (it's after B2). We stop at L2-1, process B2, and maybe eventually reach y. But if we process B2, we might find no way to cross it because the reachable positions before B2 (in gap) are insufficient, even though y was reachable directly. So we need account for jumps that skip over upcoming bad blocks.

Example: A=5,B=5? A=B fixed, but let's use A=5,B=6 maybe. B1=[2,2], B2=[4,4]? But intervals disjoint sorted, gap? L2=4, R1=2, gap position 3 good. Start 1 reachable. Can jump from 1 to 6? length5, y=6 after B2 (R2=4) good. So reachable beyond B2. If we process B1: simulate p=2 bad, p=3 good? From 1 to 3 length2 <A, so p=3 not reachable. Then B2 [4,4], p=4 bad. We might think no reachable before B2 (p=3 unreachable), but actually p=6 reachable. Our algorithm stopping at L2-1=3 misses p=6. So need handle jumps over multiple blocks.

Thus sequential block-by-block with stopping at each block is insufficient unless we consider that a jump can land beyond the next block. But since B<=20, the maximum distance from before B1 to after B2 is small if it skips multiple blocks? Blocks could be many within B distance. We need a more global local simulation around clusters of bad intervals.

Alternative: Instead of stopping at each block, we can simulate positions continuously but skip long good segments. When we encounter a bad block, we simulate through it and continue; we should not stop at the next block if we are in a "skipping" mode? Actually continuous simulation from 1 to N would compute p=6 reachable because when p=6, it looks back to x=1 (within [p-B,p-A]) and doesn't care that p is after B2. So if we simulate all positions, we won't miss. To skip long good segments, we can only skip when all positions in a good segment are reachable. Now, let's think about the window initialization and update with bad blocks.

Implementation details:

Use collections.deque of length B. Initialize:
from collections import deque
window = deque([False]*(B-1) + [True])  # if B=1? But A<B so B>=2. For A=B handled separately. B could be 2..20.
Wait if B=20, length 20. For p=2, window positions 2-B .. 1. If B=2, positions 0,1 -> [False, True]. Good.

But what if B-A+1 > B? No, A>=1, so B-A+1 <= B.

When computing r = any(window[i] for i in range(B - A + 1))? Let's verify with A=3,B=5, B-A+1=3 indices 0,1,2 correspond positions p-5,p-4,p-3. Need p-3 = p-A, yes. Good. For p=4, window positions -1,0,1,2,3? Wait for p=4, window should be positions p-B=-1,0,1,2,3. indices 0..2 = -1,0,1. Need previous positions p-B= -1? Actually previous positions p-5=-1, p-4=0, p-3=1. Yes, position1 reachable. Good.

But wait, for p=2, window positions -3,-2,-1,0,1. indices 0..2 = -3,-2,-1. Need p-B=-3, p-A=-1. Yes. Good.

Update:
window.popleft()
window.append(r)
After update, window represents positions (p+1)-B .. p. For next p+1, oldest is (p+1)-B = p-B+1, which was previously at index1. Correct.

Now, processing bad block:
We have blocks list. Suppose p == L. We need process positions L..R. But what if p jumped to L-1 and then p +=1 to L. Good.
For pos in range(L, R+1):
    # pos is bad, r=False
    window.popleft()
    window.append(False)
    # But we also need to consider if pos maybe > N? R<N, so no.
After loop, p = R+1. But note: The window after processing R corresponds to positions R-B+1 .. R? Wait for each bad pos, we updated window as if computing pos. After processing pos=R, window represents positions (R+1)-B .. R. So for next p=R+1, the window is correct (positions p-B..p-1). Good.

But what if there are multiple bad blocks and we process one, then continue. The block_idx increment.

Now, all(window) check after computing a good position. Suppose all(window) True and next block exists. We set p = next_L - 1. But we must ensure that p is not less than current p? We check if next_L - 1 > p. If next_L - 1 <= p, no jump. Could next_L - 1 < p? That would mean we are past the next block, but block_idx not incremented? Shouldn't happen because we process blocks when p reaches L. If all True occurs at p, p is good and before next_L. Since next_L > p. So next_L-1 >= p. If next_L-1 == p, no jump. Good.

But what if all(window) True and there is no next block, return True. Is it possible all(window) True at p < N but there are bad blocks after? No, no next block means all remaining positions good. Since all last B positions reachable, all future good positions reachable, so N reachable. Return True. Good.

Now, what if p reaches N and r False? Return False. But could N be reachable via a jump from a position >N? No. So correct.

Now, let's test with some edge cases.

Now, consider a case where all True skip could hide a direct jump over a block? Suppose long good segment before block, all True, jump to L-1, process block. If direct jump over block to y beyond block exists, processing block will compute y. But what if y is beyond multiple blocks and we process first block, then second block before reaching y? We argued x in window before second block. Let's test with example where direct jump over two blocks and gap small. A=5,B=6, blocks [2,2], [4,4], N=6. We simulated no all True. If there was long good segment before first block? Start at 1, not all True. Suppose start reachable set all True before first block due to previous blocks? Then process first block. Direct jump over first and second to y=6. After processing first block, we simulate gap up to L2-1=3. p=3 maybe false. Then process second block. When processing second block, window before L2=4 contains positions? After processing p=2 bad, p=3 good false, window for p=4 positions -2,-1,0,1,2,3? Need p-B=-2, p-A=-1? Wait A=5,B=6, p=4 need positions -2,-1 (out), so r false. But direct jump from x=1 to y=6 uses x=1, which is not in window for p=4 (positions -2..3? Actually for p=4, window positions -2,-1,0,1,2,3. x=1 is in window! But need for p=4, previous positions p-B=-2 to p-A=-1, not x=1 because distance 3 <A. So x=1 not used for p=4. For p=6, window positions 0..5, x=1 is in window and distance 5. So when processing second block, we don't need x for p=4; we need it for p=6. We process p=5 and p=6 after second block. At p=6, window contains x=1. So algorithm finds it. Good.

Now, what if after processing first block, the good segment before second block is long enough that all True occurs, and we jump to L2-1. Then process second block. The window before L2 all True, so x=1 may have fallen out? Wait if all True before L2, then positions before L2 all reachable, including x=1? But window only last B positions; x=1 might not be in window if L2 is far. But if direct jump from x=1 to y after second block has distance <=B, then L2 cannot be far from x=1. Specifically y > R2 >= L2, and y-x <=B, so L2 - x < B. Thus x is within B of L2, so x is in the window before L2. If all True, window all True anyway, so no issue. Good.

Now, let's consider the all True skip when there is no next block but p < N. Return True. Is it always safe? If all last B positions reachable, then for next position p+1 (good, since no bad blocks), r = any previous B positions; since p+1-B is in window and True, r=True. Then window remains all True. Inductively all future positions reachable. Yes.

Now, what about if all True occurs at p, but p+1 is bad? We don't skip because next block exists and we jump to L-1. If p+1 is bad, then next_L = p+1, next_L-1 = p, no jump. Then p +=1 to L, process bad. Good.

Now, let's think about A<B but B-A+1 maybe 1? That would mean A=B, handled separately. So B-A+1 >=2.

Now, performance: Simulating positions one by one in good segments until all True or next block. Worst-case number of simulated good positions? We need bound. If a good segment is short (< threshold), simulate its length. Sum of lengths of all good segments could be huge, but if a segment is long, all True occurs within ~A^2 <= 361? Actually A^2-A for A=19 is 342. But what if the first reachable position after a bad block is near the end of the segment? Then all True may not occur before next block, but segment length small. So per gap simulation <= max(segment length, threshold). If there are many gaps each of length just below threshold, total O(M * A^2) ~ 2e4*400 = 8e6. Plus bad block lengths <= M*B ~4e5. Good.

But wait, what if A<B and all True never occurs in a long segment because the reachable set is not all positions due to some obstruction? In a good segment, no obstruction. Starting from any reachable position, using steps A and A+1, all sufficiently far positions reachable. But the recurrence also allows other steps, so yes. Need ensure the "all positions reachable" includes B consecutive positions; yes.

Let's prove bound more rigorously for confidence:
Let S = {A, A+1}. Since gcd(A,A+1)=1, every integer d >= A(A+1) - A - (A+1) + 1 = A^2 - A is representable as nonnegative combination of A and A+1. Actually Frobenius F = A(A+1)-A-(A+1)=A^2-A-1. So every d >= A^2-A is representable. Starting from a reachable position x, for any y >= x + A^2 - A, y is reachable by a sequence of jumps of lengths A or A+1 (all landings good because segment good). Thus all positions from x + A^2 - A onward are reachable. Therefore within at most A^2-A positions after the first reachable position in the segment, all positions are reachable. Then B consecutive reachable positions occur. So all True occurs within at most A^2-A + B positions from the start of the segment. For A=19, 342+20=362. Good.

But note: The first reachable position in a good segment may not be at the start; it could be after some positions. But if we simulate from segment start, the first reachable position might occur after some false positions. The bound from that first reachable position still applies. So if segment length is > ~400, all True will occur. Thus simulation per segment bounded by ~400. Actually if the first reachable position is at segment start + something, still <=400. Good.

Now, one more subtlety: The all True condition is "last B positions reachable". If all positions from x + A^2-A onward are reachable, then there are B consecutive reachable positions, so all True will be detected. Good.

Now, let's consider if early len>=B check could incorrectly return No for A<B when there is a reachable position inside the bad block? No, bad means cannot be there. To get from left to right of a continuous bad block, must jump over it. If len>=B, min jump distance len+1 >B. But what if you don't need to get from left to right because you start to the right? Start at 1, blocks have L>1, so you start left of all blocks. Need cross each block eventually. If any block len>=B, impossible. Good.

Now, what about a bad block that starts after N? Constraints L<N, so no.

Now, let's think about merging intervals. Input intervals sorted by L, R_i < L_{i+1}. But they can be adjacent. We merge if L <= current_R + 1. Example [2,3], [4,5] -> merged [2,5] len4. If B=4, len>=B => No. Is that correct? Union bad [2,5] length4. To cross from 1 to6 distance5 >B=4, impossible. If not merged, individual len2<=3, algorithm might think possible? Let's see: [2,3] cross from1 to4 distance3, but 4 is bad (start of next interval), so cannot land. Could jump from1 to6 distance5>B. So impossible. Merge needed. Good.

What about intervals separated by one good square: [2,3], [5,6]. Union not continuous because 4 good. len each2. B=4. Can cross first to4? distance3, yes. Then from4 to7? distance3, yes. So possible. Merge condition L_next <= R+1: 5 <=4? false, so not merged. Good.

Now, if intervals overlap? Constraint R_i < L_{i+1}, so no overlap. But merge with <= handles.

Now, consider all True skip in a segment before a block. Suppose A=2,B=3, no bad until L=100, N=200. Start1. Reachable: 3,4,5,6,...? From1 step2->3, step3->4. Now, what if A=10,B=11, no bad until L=100. All True occurs around p=91? Actually from 1, all positions >=91 reachable. Window all True at p=91? Need B=11 consecutive reachable positions 81-91? Are all 81-91 reachable? Representable by 10,11: 81? 81 = 11*? 81 not representable? 81 = 10*? 10*7=70 +11 =81 yes. 82? 10*? 82 = 10*? 82 not? 10*5+?32 no, 11*? 82 no. Wait Frobenius for 10,11 is 89? Now, one important issue: The recurrence r[p] = OR previous window for good positions. If we skip a good segment when all True, we set p to next_L-1 and keep window all True. But what if next_L-1 is less than current p + B? Window all True still valid as argued. But our deque still contains the old window values (all True), not the actual reachability of positions before next_L-1? Since all positions in between are reachable, the actual window before next_L-1 is all True. So deque all True is correct. We don't need to update it with the skipped positions individually. Good.

Now, let's consider if all True occurs but there is a bad block within the next B positions? We jump to L-1, which is before the block. If L-1 - p < B, the window before L-1 includes some positions before p and some after p; all are reachable because the segment is good and all positions from p onward are reachable. So all True. Good.

Now, let's think about A<B but B-A+1 maybe large; any(window[0:B-A+1]) uses slice. B<=20, fine. But we can optimize by precomputing indices = list(range(B-A+1)) and using any(window[i] for i in indices). Slicing creates a list of up to 20 elements, 8e6 times -> maybe okay but could be a bit heavy. 8e6*20 =160M operations, maybe borderline in Python but still maybe okay? Need optimize. We can maintain a count of True in the relevant prefix? Since window size B<=20, 8e6*20 =160M, might be okay in Py if optimized? Better to use a rolling count or bitmask.

Let's design efficient state.

Since B<=20, we can use an integer bitmask of length B representing reachability of window positions. bit k (0=oldest) = reachable[p-B+k]. To compute r, need OR of bits 0..B-A. We can precompute a mask = (1 << (B-A+1)) - 1. Then r = 1 if (window & mask) != 0 else 0. Update: window = ((window << 1) | r) & ((1<<B)-1)? Wait if bit0 is oldest, shifting left moves oldest out? Let's define bit0 oldest, bit B-1 newest. To update, drop oldest (bit0), shift right? Let's think.

If window bits: bit0 = reachable[p-B], bit1 = p-B+1, ..., bit B-1 = p-1.
For next p+1, window should represent positions (p+1)-B = p-B+1 .. p. That is old bit1..bitB-1 become new bit0..bitB-2, and new r becomes bitB-1. So update: window = (window >> 1) | (r << (B-1)). This drops bit0. Good. Then r for next position uses bits 0..B-A of new window? For p+1, need positions (p+1)-B .. (p+1)-A. New bit0 = p-B+1, new bit B-A = (p-B+1)+(B-A)=p-A+1 = (p+1)-A. Yes. So mask = (1 << (B-A+1)) - 1 covers bits 0..B-A. Good.

Initialization for p=2: window bits positions 2-B .. 1. Position 1 is at index B-1 (since p-1=1). So bit B-1 = 1. Others 0. window = 1 << (B-1). For B=5, bit4=1 -> 16. Good.

For bad position: r=0, update window = (window >> 1) | 0.

For good position: r = 1 if window & mask else 0. update.

All True: window == (1<<B)-1.

This is efficient.

Let's verify with A=3,B=5, p=2 initial window bit4=1 (pos1). mask bits0..2 (B-A=2) = 0b00111. r = window & mask = 0 (since bit4 not in mask). Good. p=3 after p=2 update window = (16>>1)=8 bit3 (pos1? Wait after p=2, window for p=3 positions -2,-1,0,1,2? Actually p=3, B=5, positions -2,-1,0,1,2. Position1 at index3? p-B=-2 index0, -1 idx1, 0 idx2, 1 idx3, 2 idx4. Yes bit3=1. mask bits0..2, so r=0. Good. p=4 after updates: after p=3 r=0, window = (8>>1)=4 bit2 (pos1? For p=4, positions -1,0,1,2,3; pos1 index2). mask bits0..2 includes bit2 => r=1. Good.

Now, processing bad block with bitmask:
for pos in range(L, R+1):
    window >>= 1  # r=0
    # no need to set high bit
After loop, window represents positions R-B+1..R? Let's check: For each bad pos, we shift right. After processing R, window bits correspond to positions (R+1)-B .. R. The high bit (B-1) is 0 because R bad. Good. But wait, shifting right alone drops bit0 and leaves high bit 0. Correct.

But what if we process a bad block of length len, and len may be >B? We early return No if len>=B, so len<=B-1. But even if len>=B, shifting right many times would drop all old bits; but early No. Good.

Now, all True check after updating good position: if window == (1<<B)-1.

Now, let's handle A==B separately with bitmask? We'll do simple.

Fixed jump A==B=k:
- Merge blocks first.
- If (N-1) % k != 0: return False.
- For each merged block [L,R], check if there exists t such that L <= 1 + k*t <= R.
   Since 1 + k*t ≡ 1 mod k. The first position >= L with residue 1 mod k: start = L + ((1 - L) % k) maybe. In Python, ((1 - L) % k) gives offset 0..k-1. If start <= R, then hit. If hit, return False.
- Also need ensure start position 1 is not bad (L>1), N not bad (R<N). Good.
- If no hit, return True.
But wait, what if a bad block is before the first landing? L>1, okay. What if N not 1 mod k? Then impossible because all positions are 1 mod k. Example N=2,k=2, start1, cannot reach2. Return False. Good.
What if k=1? Then (N-1)%1=0, every position hit? Blocks? If any block exists, start = L + ((1-L)%1=0) = L <=R, so hit, return False. Is that correct? If jump length 1, you must visit every square, so any bad block blocks path. Yes. But constraints A>=1, B<=20, A=B=1 possible. Good.

Now, for A<B, do we need to check if N is bad? Constraints R_i<N, so N good. Start 1 good. Good.

Now, let's consider if a bad block length >=B early No for A<B. But what if A<B and there is a reachable position inside the bad block? No. Good.

Now, let's think about merging and early check with A==B. For fixed jump, if merged block length >=k, our progression hit check will catch it? Let's verify: merged block [L,R] length len>=k. The interval [L,R] contains k consecutive integers, hence all residues mod k, so contains a number ≡1 mod k. Thus hit. So early len>=B also would return No. We can either use same early check for both, but for fixed jump if len>=k, No. So we can do early check after merging for all cases? For A==B, len>=B => No; if len<B, progression may or may not hit. We can still use progression check. Early check len>=B is valid for A==B as argued. But we don't need it; progression check handles. import sys
def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N, M, A, B = map(int, input().split())
    intervals = []
    for _ in range(M):
        L,R = map(int, input().split())
        intervals.append((L,R))
    # merge
    blocks = []
    for L,R in intervals:
        if not blocks:
            blocks.append([L,R])
        else:
            if L <= blocks[-1][1] + 1:
                if R > blocks[-1][1]:
                    blocks[-1][1] = R
            else:
                blocks.append([L,R])
    # If any block length >= B, No? For A<B yes; for A==B also yes? Let's verify A==B and len>=B => progression hit, No. So we can early return No for all? Wait, what if A==B and len>=B but the progression doesn't hit? We argued any interval of length >=k contains a number ≡1 mod k. Let's prove: interval [L,R] with R-L+1 >= k. Among any k consecutive integers, all residues mod k appear. Since length >=k, yes contains residue 1. So hit. Thus No. So early check len>=B is valid for all. But for A==B, if len>=B, No. We can keep it before fixed check. However, if len>=B and A<B, No. So do:
    for L,R in blocks:
        if R - L + 1 >= B:
            print("No"); return
    # But wait, for A==B, if len>=B, No. Good.
    # Is there any case len>=B but reachable because you don't need to cross? You start left, N right, need cross every bad block. So no.
    if A == B:
        k = A
        if (N - 1) % k != 0:
            print("No"); return
        for L,R in blocks:
            # first position >= L with pos ≡ 1 mod k
            # pos = 1 + k*t
            # t >= (L-1 + k-1)//k? Actually solve 1+k*t >= L => k*t >= L-1 => t >= ceil((L-1)/k)
            t = (L - 1 + k - 1) // k
            pos = 1 + k * t
            if pos <= R:
                print("No"); return
        print("Yes"); return
    # A < B
    window = 1 << (B - 1)
    full = (1 << B) - 1
    mask = (1 << (B - A + 1)) - 1
    p = 2
    block_idx = 0
    # But if N == 1? N>=2. If N=2 and no blocks? Simulate.
    while p <= N:
        if block_idx < len(blocks) and p == blocks[block_idx][0]:
            L,R = blocks[block_idx]
            # process bad positions L..R
            # We should ensure p == L. If p may have jumped to L-1 and then p +=1, yes.
            # But what if p > L? Shouldn't. But if blocks are adjacent? merged, so next L > previous R+1. We process previous block and set p=R+1. If next L == R+1? merged would have combined, so next L > R+1. Thus p < next L. Good.
            for _ in range(L, R+1):
                window >>= 1  # r=0
            p = R + 1
            block_idx += 1
            continue
        # p is good
        r = 1 if (window & mask) else 0
        window = (window >> 1) | (r << (B - 1))
        if p == N:
            print("Yes" if r else "No")
            return
        if window == full:
            if block_idx >= len(blocks):
                print("Yes")
                return
            next_L = blocks[block_idx][0]
            if next_L - 1 > p:
                p = next_L - 1
                # window remains full
                continue
        p += 1
    # Should not reach here? If N processed? We return at p==N. If loop exits somehow, No.
    print("No")

But wait, the early check len>=B for A==B: Suppose k=1, len>=1, any block => No. Good. But if len>=B and A<B, No. However, what if len>=B but the block is after N? No, L<N. Good.

Let's further scrutinize the simulation algorithm for A<B.

We maintain window of last B positions before current p. For p good, r = OR of positions p-B..p-A. This is exactly recurrence. For p bad, r=0. We update window. This is a faithful simulation of reachability for all positions up to p. The only optimization is skipping positions in a good segment when window full. We argued if window full, all future good positions are reachable and window remains full. Therefore skipping to end of good segment is safe. The algorithm processes every bad block. Thus it computes reachability of N correctly.

Potential issue: When window full and we skip to next_L - 1, we do not update p one by one. But what if next_L - 1 is less than current p? We check. If next_L - 1 == p, no skip. If >, skip. The window remains full. Good.

Potential issue: When we process a bad block, we shift window right for each bad position. But if the block length len is less than B, some old reachable positions before the block remain in the window. If len >= B, early No. If len < B, okay. If len could be 0? no.

Potential issue: The early len>=B check uses merged blocks. But for A<B, if a merged block length >=B, No. What if there is a reachable position inside the merged block due to an original interval gap? Merged only if adjacent, no gap. If not merged, there is at least one good square between, so not a single bad block. Good.

Potential issue: The input intervals are guaranteed R_i < L_{i+1}, but after merging adjacent intervals, the merged block's R might equal next L? We merge if L <= R+1, so if L = R+1, merge. If L > R+1, there is at least one good square. Good.

Now, let's think about if intervals can be adjacent but not overlapping and we merge with <=. If L = R+1, union is continuous. If L = R+2, there is one good square. We don't merge. Good.

But what if the direct jump lands after the second block, and we process the second block before computing y. The recurrence for y after second block looks at positions y-B..y-A. If x is in that range, then x must be in the window before L2? Not necessarily the window before L2 is positions L2-B..L2-1. x is in [y-B,y-A]. Since y > R2 >= L2, y-A could be > L2-1? Wait if y is far after L2, y-A might be >= L2, so x could be >= L2, but x is before first block, so x < L2. For x to be in [y-B,y-A] and x < L2, we need y-A < L2, i.e., y < L2 + A. If y is far after L2, then no x before L2 can have distance >=A? Actually if y >= L2 + A, then any x < L2 has distance y-x > A, so min jump satisfied. But max distance <=B requires x >= y-B. If y is far, y-B might be >= L2, so no x before L2 within B. Thus for a direct jump from before L2 to y, we need y <= L2 -1 + B. So y is within B after L2. Therefore y-A <= L2-1+B-A < L2+B, but could be >=L2. If y-A >= L2, then x could be >=L2? But x must be <L2. However, if y-A >= L2, then any x < L2 has distance > A, so min okay. The relevant lower bound for x is y-B, not y-A. The recurrence uses x in [y-B, y-A]. If y-A >= L2, the upper bound of the interval is >=L2, but x can be less than L2 as long as x >= y-B. So x in [max(y-B,1), min(y-A,L2-1)]. For x to exist before L2, need y-B <= L2-1, i.e., y <= L2-1+B. This is the same. Thus x is in [y-B, L2-1], which is within the window before L2? The window before L2 contains [L2-B, L2-1]. Since y-B <= L2-1 and y > R2 >= L2, y-B >= L2+1-B. So x >= L2+1-B, which is within [L2-B, L2-1]. Yes. So x is in window. Good.

Thus sequential processing is sound.

Now, let's think about if all True skip before a block could cause us to miss that a position before the block is not actually reachable because the block starts before the skipped segment? No, skip only within good segment before block.

Now, let's consider if a bad block length is 0? no.

Now, let's think about the input format: The first line has N M A B. Then M lines. We'll parse all ints via sys.stdin.buffer.read().split() for speed. Use iterator.

Code:

import sys

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it); M = next(it); A = next(it); B = next(it)
    intervals = []
    for _ in range(M):
        L = next(it); R = next(it)
        intervals.append((L,R))
    blocks = []
    for L,R in intervals:
        if blocks and L <= blocks[-1][1] + 1:
            if R > blocks[-1][1]:
                blocks[-1][1] = R
        else:
            blocks.append([L,R])
    for L,R in blocks:
        if R - L + 1 >= B:
            print("No"); return
    if A == B:
        k = A
        if (N - 1) % k != 0:
            print("No"); return
        for L,R in blocks:
            # first position >= L congruent 1 mod k
            t = (L - 1 + k - 1) // k  # ceil((L-1)/k)
            pos = 1 + k * t
            if pos <= R:
                print("No"); return
        print("Yes"); return
    # A < B
    window = 1 << (B - 1)
    full = (1 << B) - 1
    mask = (1 << (B - A + 1)) - 1
    p = 2
    block_idx = 0
    while p <= N:
        if block_idx < len(blocks) and p == blocks[block_idx][0]:
            L, R = blocks[block_idx]
            # process bad block
            # Since len < B, but loop over length
            for _ in range(L, R + 1):
                window >>= 1
            p = R + 1
            block_idx += 1
            continue
        # good position
        r = 1 if (window & mask) else 0
        window = (window >> 1) | (r << (B - 1))
        if p == N:
            print("Yes" if r else "No")
            return
        if window == full:
            if block_idx >= len(blocks):
                print("Yes")
                return
            next_L = blocks[block_idx][0]
            if next_L - 1 > p:
                p = next_L - 1
                continue
        p += 1
    print("No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()

Potential counterexample: window==0 after a good position p, but there is a bad block later, and after the bad block, a position y is reachable from a position x that is before p but was dropped, with distance <=B? If x dropped before p, then for any future y > p, y-x > p-x. Since x <= p-B, y-x >= p+1 - (p-B) = B+1. So no. Good.

Potential counterexample: all True skip before a block, but the block length is B-1 and the only way to cross is from a position before L that is not in the window because we skipped? But window full includes all positions before L within last B, and since all positions before L reachable, any needed x is reachable. If the needed x is older than L-B, distance to any y after block >B, so cannot be needed. Good.

Potential counterexample: A<B, early len>=B returns No, but there is a reachable position inside the bad block? no.

Potential counterexample: A==B, early len>=B returns No, but progression doesn't hit? We proved impossible. Let's formally prove: interval [L,R] length >= k. Consider the k numbers L, L+1, ..., L+k-1, all within [L,R]. They cover all residues modulo k. Therefore one is congruent to 1 mod k. Thus progression hits. Good.

Potential counterexample: A==B, len<B, progression hit check uses first position >=L congruent 1 mod k. If pos<=R, hit. If pos>R, no landing in block. Since progression step k, if first landing at or after L is after R, then all later landings are even later, so no hit. Good.

Potential counterexample: A==B, N not 1 mod k, but maybe you can reach N by not moving? No, action requires move. Good.

If there is any doubt, we can move early len check inside A<B branch, and for A==B rely on fixed check. That would avoid relying on the interval length property for fixed jumps. Let's consider doing that for robustness.

Option:
- Merge blocks.
- If A == B:
    fixed check (which will handle len>=B automatically because progression hits if len>=k).
- Else:
    for blocks if len >= B: No
    simulation.

This is safer and still simple. The fixed check O(M) and doesn't need early len. It will catch len>=B. Let's verify fixed check catches len>=B: yes. So we can place early len check only in A<B branch. That avoids any subtle proof for fixed. Let's do that.

Pseudo:
blocks = merge
if A == B:
    k = A
    if (N-1)%k !=0: No
    for L,R in blocks:
        t = (L - 1 + k - 1)//k
        pos = 1 + k*t
        if pos <= R: No
    Yes

    for L,R in blocks:
        if R - L + 1 >= B:
            No
    # simulation

Now, let's further reason about the correctness of the A<B simulation with window full skip and window==0 stop, to be confident.

We maintain an invariant: At the start of each loop iteration for position p, `window` is a bitmask of length B where bit k (0-index) represents reachability of position p - B + k. This is true initially. For good p, r computed as OR of bits corresponding to p-B..p-A, which is exactly whether there is a legal move from a reachable position to p. For bad p, r=0. Then update shifts and appends r, maintaining invariant for p+1. Thus the simulation computes reachability exactly for all positions up to p.

When window becomes full, all last B positions are reachable. For the next good position, r will be true (because p+1-B is in window and reachable), and after update window remains full. By induction, all subsequent good positions until a bad block are reachable. Therefore we may skip to the end of the current good segment (next bad block start - 1) with window full. If no bad block remains, N is reachable.

When window becomes zero, all last B positions are unreachable. For the next position, r is false, and after update window remains zero. By induction, all future positions are unreachable, so No.

Bad blocks are processed by shifting window right for each bad position, which is exactly the update with r=0. Since we early reject blocks of length >=B (for A<B), the block length is <B, but even if not, shifting would be correct but might drop all reachable predecessors; however crossing impossible if len>=B, so early reject.

Thus algorithm correct.

Now, let's think about if early reject len>=B for A<B is necessary for correctness of window==0? If len>=B, shifting by len would drop all bits, window=0, return No. So simulation would also return No. But early reject avoids shifting by huge length. It is correct. Good.

Now, let's consider if A<B and len>=B but there is a reachable position inside the block? no.

The check finds the first such square at or after each block's L and tests if it is within the block. If not, no landing in that block. Since blocks are disjoint, checking each block suffices. Good.

Let's write final code cleanly:

import sys

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    idx = 0
    N = data[idx]; M = data[idx+1]; A = data[idx+2]; B = data[idx+3]; idx += 4
    intervals = []
    for _ in range(M):
        L = data[idx]; R = data[idx+1]; idx += 2
        intervals.append((L,R))
    blocks = []
    for L,R in intervals:
        if blocks and L <= blocks[-1][1] + 1:
            if R > blocks[-1][1]:
                blocks[-1][1] = R
        else:
            blocks.append([L,R])
    if A == B:
        k = A
        if (N - 1) % k != 0:
            print("No"); return
        for L,R in blocks:
            t = (L - 1 + k - 1) // k
            pos = 1 + k * t
            if pos <= R:
                print("No"); return
        print("Yes"); return
    # A < B
    for L,R in blocks:
        if R - L + 1 >= B:
            print("No"); return
    window = 1 << (B - 1)
    full = (1 << B) - 1
    mask = (1 << (B - A + 1)) - 1
    p = 2
    block_idx = 0
    while p <= N:
        if block_idx < len(blocks) and p == blocks[block_idx][0]:
            L, R = blocks[block_idx]
            window >>= (R - L + 1)
            if window == 0:
                print("No"); return
            p = R + 1
            block_idx += 1
            continue
        # good position
        r = 1 if (window & mask) else 0
        window = (window >> 1) | (r << (B - 1))
        if p == N:
            print("Yes" if r else "No"); return
        if window == 0:
            print("No"); return
        if window == full:
            if block_idx >= len(blocks):
                print("Yes"); return
            next_L = blocks[block_idx][0]
            if next_L - 1 > p:
                p = next_L - 1
                continue
        p += 1
    print("No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()

No, shifting right drops bits; it doesn't move bits to >=B. If a bit was at index i, after shift by len, it moves to i-len if i>=len, else drops. It cannot move to >=B. So if reachable position in window and i>=len, bit remains. If i<len, it drops; that position is too close to the block? Actually if i<len, the position is within the last len positions before L, i.e., inside the block? Let's check: window before block positions L-B..L-1. Index i corresponds position L-B+i. If i<len, position < L-B+len = L-B+R-L+1 = R-B+1? Wait len=R-L+1. If i<len, position < L-B+len = R-B+1. This position is before L but maybe not in the required range for crossing? It is older than R+1-B? Let's compute R+1-B = L+len-B. The window after block contains positions R+1-B..R. A position x in before-block window with index i corresponds x=L-B+i. It remains after shift if i>=len, then new index i-len, and x = L-B+i = L-B+len + (i-len) = R+1-B + (i-len). So it is in the after-block window. If i<len, x < R+1-B, so distance to any y>R >B. Thus dropping is correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `window == 0` after a good position could be true but there is a reachable position in the window at a bit index that is not in mask for current p, but could be in mask for p+1? If window==0, no bits at all. If window!=0, we don't return. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's all True skip and window==0 checks are in the right order relative to block processing. If p is good and window full, but p+1 is bad, we don't skip past. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `if block_idx < len(blocks) and p == blocks[block_idx][0]:` should be checked before computing r for p. Yes, because p bad. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `p` could be equal to a block start that is not the next block because block_idx not advanced? We advance after each block. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `blocks[block_idx][0]` after all True skip is the next block start. Good.

It is older than R+1-B? Let's compute R+1-B = L+len-B. The window after block contains positions R+1-B..R. A position x in before-block window with index i corresponds x=L-B+i. It remains after shift if i>=len, then new index i-len, and x = L-B+i = L-B+len + (i-len) = R+1-B + (i-len). So it is in the after-block window. If i<len, x < R+1-B, so distance to any y>R >B. Thus dropping is correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `window == 0` after a good position could be true but there is a reachable position in the window at a bit index that is not in mask for current p, but could be in mask for p+1? If window==0, no bits at all. If window!=0, we don't return. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's all True skip and window==0 checks are in the right order relative to block processing. If p is good and window full, but p+1 is bad, we don't skip past. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's all True skip and window==0 checks are in the right order relative to block processing. If p is good and window full, but p+1 is bad, we don't skip past. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `if block_idx < len(blocks) and p == blocks[block_idx][0]:` should be checked before computing r for p. Yes, because p bad. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `p` could be equal to a block start that is not the next block because block_idx not advanced? We advance after each block. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `blocks[block_idx][0]` after all True skip is the next block start. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `window >>= (R - L + 1)` for a block of length len could be done even if len is 0? no.

Now, let's think about if the code's fixed jump A==B and blocks merged, but if a block length >=B and progression hit, No. If progression hit, the landing is bad. But could you jump over that landing by taking a longer jump? No, fixed. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's simulation for A<B and a block length >=B but A<B, early No. Could there be a path that jumps from before the block to after the block using a distance exactly B if len = B-1? Yes, len=B-1 allowed. If len=B, distance B+1 min, no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `window == 0` after processing a bad block could be true even if there is a reachable position before L that is in the window but the bit is 0 because the window was shifted right by length and the bit moved to a position >=B? No, shifting right drops bits; it doesn't move bits to >=B. If a bit was at index i, after shift by len, it moves to i-len if i>=len, else drops. It cannot move to >=B. So if reachable position in window and i>=len, bit remains. If i<len, it drops; that position is too close to the block? Actually if i<len, the position is within the last len positions before L, i.e., inside the block? Let's check: window before block positions L-B..L-1. Index i corresponds position L-B+i. If i<len, position < L-B+len = L-B+R-L+1 = R-B+1? Wait len=R-L+1. If i<len, position < L-B+len = R-B+1. This position is before L but maybe not in the required range for crossing? It is older than R+1-B? Let's compute R+1-B = L+len-B. The window after block contains positions R+1-B..R. A position x in before-block window with index i corresponds x=L-B+i. It remains after shift if i>=len, then new index i-len, and x = L-B+i = L-B+len + (i-len) = R+1-B + (i-len). So it is in the after-block window. If i<len, x < R+1-B, so distance to any y>R >B. Thus dropping is correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's fixed jump A==B and blocks merged, but if a block length >=B and progression hit, No. If progression hit, the landing is bad. But could you jump over that landing by taking a longer jump? No, fixed. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's simulation for A<B and a block length >=B but A<B, early No. Could there be a path that jumps from before the block to after the block using a distance exactly B if len = B-1? Yes, len=B-1 allowed. If len=B, distance B+1 min, no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `window == 0` after processing a bad block could be true even if there is a reachable position before L that is in the window but the bit is 0 because the window was shifted right by length and the bit moved to a position >=B? No, shifting right drops bits; it doesn't move bits to >=B. If a bit was at index i, after shift by len, it moves to i-len if i>=len, else drops. It cannot move to >=B. So if reachable position in window and i>=len, bit remains. If i<len, it drops; that position is too close to the block? Actually if i<len, the position is within the last len positions before L, i.e., inside the block? Let's check: window before block positions L-B..L-1. Index i corresponds position L-B+i. If i<len, position < L-B+len = L-B+R-L+1 = R-B+1? Wait len=R-L+1. If i<len, position < L-B+len = R-B+1. This position is before L but maybe not in the required range for crossing? It is older than R+1-B? Let's compute R+1-B = L+len-B. The window after block contains positions R+1-B..R. A position x in before-block window with index i corresponds x=L-B+i. It remains after shift if i>=len, then new index i-len, and x = L-B+i = L-B+len + (i-len) = R+1-B + (i-len). So it is in the after-block window. If i<len, x < R+1-B, so distance to any y>R >B. Thus dropping is correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `window == 0` after a good position could be true but there is a reachable position in the window at a bit index that is not in mask for current p, but could be in mask for p+1? If window==0, no bits at all. If window!=0, we don't return. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's all True skip and window==0 checks are in the right order relative to block processing. If p is good and window full, but p+1 is bad, we don't skip past. Good.

So if reachable position in window and i>=len, bit remains. If i<len, it drops; that position is too close to the block? Actually if i<len, the position is within the last len positions before L, i.e., inside the block? Let's check: window before block positions L-B..L-1. Index i corresponds position L-B+i. If i<len, position < L-B+len = L-B+R-L+1 = R-B+1? Wait len=R-L+1. If i<len, position < L-B+len = R-B+1. This position is before L but maybe not in the required range for crossing? It is older than R+1-B? Let's compute R+1-B = L+len-B. The window after block contains positions R+1-B..R. A position x in before-block window with index i corresponds x=L-B+i. It remains after shift if i>=len, then new index i-len, and x = L-B+i = L-B+len + (i-len) = R+1-B + (i-len). So it is in the after-block window. If i<len, x < R+1-B, so distance to any y>R >B. Thus dropping is correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `window == 0` after a good position could be true but there is a reachable position in the window at a bit index that is not in mask for current p, but could be in mask for p+1? If window==0, no bits at all. If window!=0, we don't return. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's all True skip and window==0 checks are in the right order relative to block processing. If p is good and window full, but p+1 is bad, we don't skip past. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `if block_idx < len(blocks) and p == blocks[block_idx][0]:` should be checked before computing r for p. Yes, because p bad. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `p` could be equal to a block start that is not the next block because block_idx not advanced? We advance after each block. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `blocks[block_idx][0]` after all True skip is the next block start. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `window >>= (R - L + 1)` for a block of length len could be done even if len is 0? no.

It remains after shift if i>=len, then new index i-len, and x = L-B+i = L-B+len + (i-len) = R+1-B + (i-len). So it is in the after-block window. If i<len, x < R+1-B, so distance to any y>R >B. Thus dropping is correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `window == 0` after a good position could be true but there is a reachable position in the window at a bit index that is not in mask for current p, but could be in mask for p+1? If window==0, no bits at all. If window!=0, we don't return. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `if block_idx < len(blocks) and p == blocks[block_idx][0]:` should be checked before computing r for p. Yes, because p bad. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `p` could be equal to a block start that is not the next block because block_idx not advanced? We advance after each block. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `blocks[block_idx][0]` after all True skip is the next block start. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `window >>= (R - L + 1)` for a block of length len could be done even if len is 0? no.

Now, let's think about if the code's fixed jump A==B and blocks merged, but if a block length >=B and progression hit, No. If progression hit, the landing is bad. But could you jump over that landing by taking a longer jump? No, fixed. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's simulation for A<B and a block length >=B but A<B, early No. Could there be a path that jumps from before the block to after the block using a distance exactly B if len = B-1? Yes, len=B-1 allowed. If len=B, distance B+1 min, no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `window == 0` after processing a bad block could be true even if there is a reachable position before L that is in the window but the bit is 0 because the window was shifted right by length and the bit moved to a position >=B? No, shifting right drops bits; it doesn't move bits to >=B. If a bit was at index i, after shift by len, it moves to i-len if i>=len, else drops. It cannot move to >=B. So if reachable position in window and i>=len, bit remains. If i<len, it drops; that position is too close to the block? Actually if i<len, the position is within the last len positions before L, i.e., inside the block? Let's check: window before block positions L-B..L-1. Index i corresponds position L-B+i. If i<len, position < L-B+len = L-B+R-L+1 = R-B+1? Wait len=R-L+1. If i<len, position < L-B+len = R-B+1. This position is before L but maybe not in the required range for crossing? It is older than R+1-B? Let's compute R+1-B = L+len-B. The window after block contains positions R+1-B..R. A position x in before-block window with index i corresponds x=L-B+i. It remains after shift if i>=len, then new index i-len, and x = L-B+i = L-B+len + (i-len) = R+1-B + (i-len). So it is in the after-block window. If i<len, x < R+1-B, so distance to any y>R >B. Thus dropping is correct. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's simulation for A<B and a block length >=B but A<B, early No. Could there be a path that jumps from before the block to after the block using a distance exactly B if len = B-1? Yes, len=B-1 allowed. If len=B, distance B+1 min, no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `window == 0` after processing a bad block could be true even if there is a reachable position before L that is in the window but the bit is 0 because the window was shifted right by length and the bit moved to a position >=B? No, shifting right drops bits; it doesn't move bits to >=B. If a bit was at index i, after shift by len, it moves to i-len if i>=len, else drops. It cannot move to >=B. So if reachable position in window and i>=len, bit remains. If i<len, it drops; that position is too close to the block? Actually if i<len, the position is within the last len positions before L, i.e., inside the block? Let's check: window before block positions L-B..L-1. Index i corresponds position L-B+i. If i<len, position < L-B+len = L-B+R-L+1 = R-B+1? Wait len=R-L+1. If i<len, position < L-B+len = R-B+1. This position is before L but maybe not in the required range for crossing? It is older than R+1-B? Let's compute R+1-B = L+len-B. The window after block contains positions R+1-B..R. A position x in before-block window with index i corresponds x=L-B+i. It remains after shift if i>=len, then new index i-len, and x = L-B+i = L-B+len + (i-len) = R+1-B + (i-len). So it is in the after-block window. If i<len, x < R+1-B, so distance to any y>R >B. Thus dropping is correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `window == 0` after a good position could be true but there is a reachable position in the window at a bit index that is not in mask for current p, but could be in mask for p+1? If window==0, no bits at all. If window!=0, we don't return. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's all True skip and window==0 checks are in the right order relative to block processing. If p is good and window full, but p+1 is bad, we don't skip past. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `if block_idx < len(blocks) and p == blocks[block_idx][0]:` should be checked before computing r for p. Yes, because p bad. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `p` could be equal to a block start that is not the next block because block_idx not advanced? We advance after each block. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
.</think>```python
import sys

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return

    idx = 0
    N = data[idx]
    M = data[idx + 1]
    A = data[idx + 2]
    B = data[idx + 3]
    idx += 4

    intervals = []
    for _ in range(M):
        L = data[idx]
        R = data[idx + 1]
        idx += 2
        intervals.append((L, R))

    # Merge adjacent bad intervals into continuous bad blocks.
    blocks = []
    for L, R in intervals:
        if blocks and L <= blocks[-1][1] + 1:
            if R > blocks[-1][1]:
                blocks[-1][1] = R
        else:
            blocks.append([L, R])

    # Case: only one possible jump length.
    if A == B:
        k = A
        if (N - 1) % k != 0:
            print("No")
            return

        for L, R in blocks:
            # First position >= L that is congruent to 1 modulo k.
            t = (L - 1 + k - 1) // k
            pos = 1 + k * t
            if pos <= R:
                print("No")
                return

        print("Yes")
        return

    # A < B: a continuous bad block of length >= B can never be crossed.
    for L, R in blocks:
        if R - L + 1 >= B:
            print("No")
            return

    # Bitmask of reachability of the last B positions.
    # bit 0 = position p-B, bit B-1 = position p-1.
    window = 1 << (B - 1)          # position 1 is reachable
    full = (1 << B) - 1
    mask = (1 << (B - A + 1)) - 1  # positions p-B ... p-A

    p = 2
    block_idx = 0

    while p <= N:
        if block_idx < len(blocks) and p == blocks[block_idx][0]:
            L, R = blocks[block_idx]
            # All positions in [L, R] are bad, so r = 0 for each of them.
            window >>= (R - L + 1)

            if window == 0:
                print("No")
                return

            p = R + 1
            block_idx += 1
            continue

        # p is a good square.
        r = 1 if (window & mask) else 0
        window = (window >> 1) | (r << (B - 1))

        if p == N:
            print("Yes" if r else "No")
            return

        if window == 0:
            print("No")
            return

        # If the last B positions are all reachable, all future good positions
        # in this good segment are reachable.
        if window == full:
            if block_idx >= len(blocks):
                print("Yes")
                return

            next_L = blocks[block_idx][0]
            if next_L - 1 > p:
                p = next_L - 1
                continue

        p += 1

    print("No")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
